Backlog: 33 reviewed, 4 promoted, 29 kept, 2 added. **Surface delta summary (across Phase 27):** `generation.nested_mutations` and `tables.<t>.nested_mutations` opt a schema into `CreateWithRelated` / `UpdateWithRelated` / `UpsertWithRelated`. **Phase 27 resumed: 27.9a (nested mutations on MySQL) complete 2026-09-23.** The `mysql` example turns the feature on and gains the nullable-FK `user_events` table. The sub-item found three generator defects and one PRD misstatement:
- has-one targets were not wired as write clients;
- E5 now refuses any junction payload column;
- the adoption could not see a lost race;
- §9.8.5 named the wrong precondition for `firstID + i`, which **FIX-222** now tracks.

The lost race was first fixed with a locking pre-SELECT. On 2026-09-23 that was withdrawn, after measurement: on an empty match, `FOR UPDATE` took a gap lock held until commit, and it blocked another connection's inserts into the edge. The adoption now counts a **verify read**: the chunk's ids that now carry `fk = parent`. That holds on every dialect, takes no lock, and costs no extra statement. By user ruling, MySQL `*Where` over- and under-reporting of `AffectedPKs` under a concurrent commit stays a recorded residual, stated in PRD §9.8.6b, not a FIX. `make check` and `make check-examples` both exit 0 (12 trees, `-race`). **Next:** **27.10** (GraphQL projection), then **27.11** (closure).

**Phase 28 (Transaction Connection Lock) closed 2026-09-22.** All 8 sub-items complete: **28.0** PRD sync — §18.5 rewritten around a per-`Tx` connection reservation and one caller rule (drain or close a result set before the next statement on that `txCtx`), with two decisions recorded (depth, not verb, sets the acquire mode; `AllowInTransaction` emits unconditionally) and the concurrent-savepoint-scope carve-out; **28.1** runtime preconditions — `tx.mu` untangled from every driver round trip and `database/stdlib`'s `rows.Next()` closing on exhaustion, with the two guarantees the mutex had been giving incidentally replaced rather than dropped; **28.2** the reservation itself — `connSem`, `txRows`, `txRow`, blocking savepoint statements, a non-blocking root teardown, and `closed` split from a `tearingDown` claim so `IsClosed()` means the teardown *completed*, with zero template edits and zero golden movement; **28.3** `Stream` refuses a transaction before any SQL or hook unless `CallOptions.AllowInTransaction` is set, the manifest's `call_options.fields` is derived from the struct's own emission conditions under a drift guard, and the MySQL version probe moved to the pool; **28.4** FIX-221's `g.SetLimit(1)` removed, with new multi-edge-inside-a-transaction reads on `mysql` and `sqlite`; **28.5** `Raw(ctx, sql, args, fn) error` — a scan callback routed through `database.QueryFunc` inside the hook chain; **28.6** the documentation sweep, which measured that MySQL does not tolerate a second statement over an open result set and marked the design doc SYNCED; **28.7** this closure.

**28.7 closure sweep (landed 2026-09-22):** `make check` clean across all 8 modules under `-race` (1m24s; longest leg `cmd/sqlgen/gen` ~54.1s); `make check-examples` clean across all 12 example modules (2m05s; longest leg `examples/graphql/tests` ~45.6s); `make test-integration` clean across all 8 modules under `-race` (1m40s; longest legs `cmd/sqlgen` ~58.5s, `cmd/sqlgen/gen` ~56.8s, `cmd/sqlgen/cli` ~43.6s), with `database/pgx` (~10.0s) and `database/stdlib` (~8.7s) running their reservation container probes rather than skipping them; no flakes, no reruns, 0 lint issues, no `DATA RACE` line in any leg. **FIX triage:** 0 entries logged against Phase 28 — every review finding across seven sub-items was fixed inside its own sub-item — and 0 carried forward; the Open section is empty. **One residual carried forward as an observation, not a FIX:** `cache.go.tmpl:1002` still registers two `OnCommit` callbacks behind one `IsClosed()` check, the split-registration window §18.6 documents (28.2 named the fix — register the group as one callback; 28.6 left it as code outside a documentation sweep).

**Surface delta summary (across Phase 28).** The obligation *"one statement at a time per transaction"* moved from the caller to `database.Tx`: a capacity-1 reservation is held by `Exec` across the driver call, by `Query` until its result set is **drained or closed**, and by `QueryRow` through `Scan`, so fanning reads out over one `txCtx` — the relationship loader, an `errgroup` in consumer code, two `Raw` calls — is safe on every dialect, and §20.5's withdrawn `errgroup` example is back and labelled **Safe**. Generated code moved in three consumer-visible ways and one internal one: `Raw` takes a scan callback and returns only `error`; `Stream` inside a transaction returns an error unless the new `CallOptions.AllowInTransaction` (on every package) is set; the relationship fan-out is unbounded again inside a transaction and serialized by the reservation; and the manifest's `call_options.fields` stops advertising a `Tx` field that never existed. The one honest regression (§4.2) is also the headline behaviour change: a caller who abandons a part-drained result set and issues another statement on the same transaction now **waits** — unbounded by default, since `TxOptions.Timeout` defaults to zero — where pgx and MySQL used to fail at once. **Release notes — a gap to close before the first release:** no Phase 28 commit carries a `!` or a `BREAKING CHANGE:` footer, so Release Please would render the `Raw` signature break (`25491a9`), the `Stream` refusal (`5dce764`) and the wait-instead-of-fail change (`23440df`) as ordinary commits; closable by amending, since the repository has no configured remote and nothing has been released — flagged rather than fixed, as with Phase 25. **Next:** **Phase 27 resumes at 27.9a** (nested mutations on MySQL — the non-`RETURNING` write path), then 27.10 (GraphQL projection) and 27.11 (closure). Outside it, the standing deferred set is 12.12 and the manifest JSON Schema v1.0.0 freeze carried since Phase 18; no FIX is open.

**28.6 (documentation sweep) complete 2026-09-22 — what the PRD and the doc comments say now matches what landed, and one claim was measured wrong.** Four templates, the runtime's doc comments, both adapters' reservation-test headers, one new codegen test, `docs/PRD.md`, `docs/design/archive/TX_CONNECTION_LOCK.md` and `docs/tracker/IMPLEMENTATION_ORDER.md`, against **87 regenerated goldens** — every changed line a comment, `expected/` matching `models/` in all twelve trees. The two decisions the task list singled out (`Begin` blocks; `AllowInTransaction` emits unconditionally) both held.

**§18.5 said the `database/sql` adapters "(MySQL, SQLite) tolerate the shape by accident". MySQL does not.** A throwaway probe on a bare `database/sql` transaction — part-read a three-row set, then a second statement — returns `busy buffer`, then `commands out of sync` on `Close` and `invalid connection` on the rollback, on the same goroutine and on another; SQLite returns nil throughout. `database/sql` serializes *calls*, which is why neither has a Go-level race, but a serialized call is not a reserved connection. Corrected in §18.5, §20.2, the `Tx` doc comment and both adapters' test headers; the design doc's banner records it against §2.2.

**The rule now reads identically everywhere it is stated**, including the generated `Querier(ctx)` (new), `Raw` and `Stream` doc comments — `Stream`'s on both the implementation and the interface, the latter found missing by the auto-review — `Stream`'s had the same gap 28.0's review found in §9.4a, naming the opt-in's obligation without saying it *is* the rule. `QueryFunc`/`QueryRowFunc` gained 28.5's caveat (the callback closes the leak, not the rule), and two runtime comments that still justified savepoint-depth blocking with the premise 28.2 disproved were rewritten. `GetMany`'s relationship sentence is gated on `HasO2MRelationships` rather than `SoftDelete`, with a failing-first test; across the moved goldens it went from 72 occurrences to 145. The PRD anchor gate passes — 28.0's "ten dangling anchors" were the ten double-hyphen `&`/em-dash anchors, which GitHub resolves. **Next:** **28.7** (closure sweep).

**28.5 (`Raw` takes a scan callback) complete 2026-09-22 — no generated code hands a live result set to a consumer any more.** One template (`client.go.tmpl`, +16/−9), one codegen unit test, one new E2E file (`postgres/tests/raw_test.go`, seven tests under `-race`), `docs/PRD.md` +5/−4, and **25 regenerated goldens** — the unit golden plus exactly the 24 `client_gen.go` the breakdown predicted, every one an identical +16/−9 diff. The terminal is the design's §3.7 body verbatim, `database.QueryFunc(ctx, database.Conn(ctx, c.querier), rawSQL, args, fn)`, so the reservation is taken and released inside the hook chain; `result.(database.Rows)` is gone from the generated tree. `make check` and `make check-examples` both exit 0, twelve trees, three dialects, `-race`.

**The callback closes the leak, not the rule — and the PRD said otherwise.** §18.5's safe-spellings table answered "Can the caller break the rule?" with **No** for both `Raw` and `QueryFunc`. `fn` is consumer code running inside an open result set — the same shape as a `Stream` loop body — so a statement it issues on the same `txCtx` before draining waits on its own rows' reservation. `TestRaw_StatementInsideFnWaitsOnItsOwnRows` measures it (a 250ms inner deadline, then `waiting for the connection held by an open result set`), the table now reads **Only from inside `fn`**, and the generated doc comment says so. §20.2's consequence list also grew a fourth entry for behavior that is genuinely new: panic recovery now wraps `fn`, so a panic while scanning comes back as an error, with the rows closed on the way out.

**Negative control:** stubbing `acquireConn`/`releaseConn` to no-ops makes the fan-out test fail with a data race plus `conn busy`, and the self-wait test fail with `conn busy` — the tests gate the reservation `Raw` now routes through. **Next:** 28.6 (documentation sweep), which also owns `docs/design/archive/TX_CONNECTION_LOCK.md:718`'s copy of the corrected table row.

**28.4 (the fan-out bound removed) complete 2026-09-22 — the generated tree now reaches the reservation on every multi-edge read inside a transaction instead of never getting there.** Two templates (`table/get.go.tmpl`, `table/client.go.tmpl`), one codegen unit test, **44 regenerated goldens**, four E2E test files (two new, two comment-only), plus `guidelines/CI.md` and `docs/tracker/IMPLEMENTATION_ORDER.md`. The golden diff is comment lines plus the three deleted bound lines and **nothing else**; no import moved, no code moved, `expected/` matches `models/` in every tree. `make check` and `make check-examples` both exit 0, twelve trees, three dialects, under `-race`.

**The task list predicted 29 goldens; the real number is 44, and the gap is the sub-item's most transferable lesson.** FIX-221 made **three** template comment edits and the first cut of 28.4 reverted one — the auto-review caught the other two. Both are consumer-facing godoc on `GetMany`, the implementation (`get.go.tmpl:133`) and the interface (`client.go.tmpl:17`), reading *"loaded in parallel via errgroup — one at a time when ctx carries a transaction"*: after the deletion that stated the opposite of both the code and §13.2 step 4, in the doc a consumer actually reads. The fifteen extra goldens exist because that sentence is gated on `{{ if .SoftDelete }}`, not on `HasO2MRelationships` — **the files carrying the bound and the files carrying the sentence about the bound are different sets**, which is exactly what made a file-count prediction hide the miss. A count is a checksum on the change you expected, not a search for the one you didn't.

**The unit gate was inverted, not deleted, and it pins adjacency rather than absence.** The old test asserted the bound's exact text. The new one asserts no `SetLimit` anywhere **and** that `errgroup.WithContext(ctx)` is immediately followed by the first `if fo.` — so a transaction-aware bound spelled some other way is caught too. Design decision 5 says the bound must not return as a belt-and-braces; absence of one string would not enforce that, and adjacency does.

**Acceptance criterion 3 said "all three dialects" and the suite covered one, which is how this sub-item found its real result.** The multi-edge-inside-a-transaction read existed only in `graphql` (pgx); `mysql` and `sqlite` had no such test at all, so removing the bound would have shipped untested on the stdlib adapter. Both trees have `assets` with three O2M edges plus an M2M, so the shape was constructible in both; each new test reads four edges inside `WithTx` and asserts **row ids, not counts**, so a mismapped edge cannot pass.

**Widening `connSem` from 1 to 64 — which stops the reservation serializing while leaving every code path identical — separates the three dialects three ways, and two are genuinely failing-first.** pgx: **113 data races** plus `conn busy`, then a hang to the 5m timeout, FIX-221's exact signature. MySQL: **zero** data races but `tx query: driver: bad connection` followed by `stdlib rollback: invalid connection`, the poisoned-transaction pair. SQLite: **passes**. The MySQL row is the finding worth carrying — `database/sql` *does* serialize access to a `Tx`'s connection, which is why there is no Go-level race, but **serialized access is not a reserved connection**: it does not stop a second statement being issued while a result set is open, the wire protocol rejects that, and the rollback cannot run on the poisoned connection either. The design doc's §2 note that "`database/sql` already serialized it internally" holds for 28.2's runtime-level fan-out and **does not** hold for the generated loader. The SQLite arm is honestly not failing-first and its comment says so rather than implying a guarantee it is not demonstrating; it stays because that tolerance is a property of the driver, not of §18.5's contract.

**One claim was written, measured, and withdrawn.** The MySQL test's first comment said it exercised 28.1's `rows.Next()`-closes-on-exhaustion fix end to end. Reverting that fix and re-running showed the test still passing — §2.11's stdlib gap is specific to *multi-result-set* statements, and the loader's reads are single-result-set. The comment now states what was measured.

**The review also surfaced 28.2 drift that forbade 28.2's own tests.** `guidelines/CI.md:495` said the `database/` engine "does **not** make concurrent *statements* on one transaction safe … so no test here may assert that it does" — which `connSem` and each adapter's `reservation_integration_test.go` now contradict outright. Rewritten rather than deleted: what remains unsafe, and still un-assertable, is opening two savepoint **scopes** on one `txCtx` concurrently, because the reservation serializes statements, not scopes. Three more stale prose sites went with it (`nested_mutations_update_test.go:83`, `IMPLEMENTATION_ORDER.md:876` and `:967`), all fixed inside the sub-item.

**Next:** **28.5** (`Raw`'s scan callback) — independent of 28.1–28.4, and it closes the reservation leak 28.2 handed forward. Then **28.6** (documentation sweep) and **28.7** (closure).

**28.3 (`Stream`'s in-transaction guard) complete 2026-09-22 — the one API the reservation makes worse now says so instead of half-working.** Four source files — `gen/context_shared.go`, `gen/templates/table/stream.go.tmpl`, `gen/templates/client.go.tmpl`, `manifest/builder.go` (+95/−10) — four tests touched, three new, and **107 regenerated goldens**. `Stream` is the only generated method that runs consumer code *inside* an open result set, so inside a transaction it holds the transaction's one connection and its reservation for the whole iteration; a write from the loop body waits on a lock the loop itself holds. `database.InTransaction(ctx) && !options.AllowInTransaction` now refuses before any SQL **and before the hook chain is built**, so a refused `Stream` fires no hooks at all — global authorization included, which is the half a "returns an error" test would miss. Both halves are gated: a codegen test pins the guard between `resolveCallOptions` and `executeQuery`, and an E2E test pins **zero statements on the wire**, observed through a pgx `QueryTracer` because a querier wrapper cannot see inside a transaction at all.

**Three open decisions, and the third is the one with a lasting effect.** *Position:* appended after `LockMode` — §9.4a deliberately does not pin it and both placements move the same goldens, so the tiebreak is that the two read-side knobs now read together. *Factoring:* the guard is **inlined**, not lifted into `shared/` — the lock-mode guard is shared because three templates call it; this one has exactly one call site and views emit no `Stream`, so a `define`/`template` round trip would buy indirection, not deduplication. *The manifest list:* rather than hand-correct a second literal that can drift again, `call_options.fields` is now **derived** from the same conditions `sharedTypeDefinitions` emits under, and **a drift guard compares the two** — `TestCallOptionsFieldsMatchGeneratedStruct` parses the `CallOptions` struct out of each example tree's own `shared_types_gen.go` and diffs it against that same tree's own emitted `manifest_gen.json`, order included, over all five manifest-emitting trees so both arms of the tenancy gate are covered. Both halves are pipeline output, so no fixture can be faithful in the test and wrong in the pipeline. Confirmed failing the moment `Tx` is put back.

**The list it replaced was wrong in three directions at once**, which is why deriving it beat retyping it: it advertised a `Tx` field the generated `CallOptions` has never carried, and omitted `FieldOptions`, `SkipTenancy` and `Tenant`. §30.4.1 elides the sub-block, so the PRD neither contradicted nor specified it — drift, not a spec change. The manifest is read by agents through MCP, where a name that resolves to nothing is worse than a missing one; that is the reasoning §30.4.1 already records for `error_sentinels`, and this is its sibling.

**The version probe is off the transaction's connection, and the E2E gate is the stall rather than the poisoned cache.** `c.querier.QueryRow(ctx, "SELECT VERSION()")` replaces `database.Conn(ctx, c.querier).QueryRow(...)` — exactly **four** `client_gen.go` files, `mysql` and `tenancy_mysql` × `expected/`/`models/`. The stall is what a test can construct without reaching into teardown: tx1 goroutine A holds tx1's reservation on an undrained result set, tx1 goroutine B issues the client's first-ever `LockForUpdateNoWait` read and so is the goroutine inside `once.Do`, and **tx2 — a different transaction on a different, idle connection** — issues its own version-gated read. With the probe on the transaction, B parks in `once.Do` and tx2's read parks behind it for no reason connected to either transaction; with it on the pool, `once` completes in milliseconds while A still holds and tx2's read runs at once. The two readers target **different rows** on purpose: both are `FOR UPDATE NOWAIT`, so sharing a row would have the queued one fail with `ER_LOCK_NOWAIT` — row contention impersonating the reservation contention under test. `mysql_version_test.go` passes unchanged in both copies, as the task list predicted.

**A test was pinning gofumpt's column alignment, and only a longer field name could reveal it.** `TestGenerate_viewOnlyTenantedPackageStillEmitsExplicitTenantOption` asserted `strings.Contains(shared, "Tenant       *uuid.UUID")` — seven literal spaces. `AllowInTransaction` is the longest name `CallOptions` has ever carried, so gofumpt re-aligned the struct and the assertion broke while the property it exists for ("the field exists and is concretely typed") still held perfectly. Now matched on a space-run regex. The class is general and worth carrying forward: a `strings.Contains` over *formatted* output silently couples a semantic assertion to column positions, and the coupling stays invisible until something widens the struct.

**The auto-review found four things and all four were fixed inside the sub-item; two are worth carrying.** **The PRD contradicted itself about the field this sub-item added** — §9.4a says `AllowInTransaction` is on *every* generated `CallOptions[FO]`, while §9.6's canonical listing, the one a reader goes to for the struct's shape, still showed seven fields and neither `AllowInTransaction` nor `Tenant`. §9.6 now carries the full shape in declaration order plus which two fields are gated and why the two method-specific ones are not; §9.4's older sketch is labelled abbreviated and points at §9.6 rather than becoming a third copy to keep in sync. **And the `Tenant` gate was a second implementation of a predicate `gen` already owns — the exact drift this sub-item was closing.** `firstTenantedEntityType` is now exported as `gen.FirstTenantedEntityType`, because it *is* the definition of "this package emits `CallOptions.Tenant`", and the manifest calls it instead of re-deriving it. The remaining two: a hand-built MCP fixture still carried `{Type: "CallOption", Fields: ["Tx"]}` — no pipeline impact, but it is how a name that resolves to nothing survives the fix to the code that emitted it, and no drift guard can see a hand-built literal.

**The MySQL probe test was a probabilistic gate, and making it deterministic made it assert the right thing.** The first cut used a 300 ms sleep to make goroutine B the one inside `once.Do` — under load main could win that race, run the probe on tx2's idle connection, and **pass against the defect**. The corrected probe runs on *the querier the client was constructed with*, so a wrapping querier sees `SELECT VERSION()` and can close a channel on it. That channel replaces the sleep as the sequencing edge — tx2 is not opened until the probe is observed, so B is necessarily the goroutine that ran it — **and is the property under test**. Against `database.Conn(ctx, c.querier)` the probe is handed the `*Tx`, the wrapper never sees it, and the test fails on a bounded wait: **confirmed failing-first at 10.03s against a surgically reverted tree**, with the diagnostic naming the cause rather than a timeout.

**Next:** **28.4** (remove FIX-221's `g.SetLimit(1)`) — unblocked since 28.2, and it is what makes the generated tree actually exercise the reservation on every multi-edge read inside a transaction. **28.5** (`Raw`'s scan callback) is independent of 28.1–28.4 and closes the reservation leak 28.2 handed forward.

**28.2 (the reservation) complete 2026-09-22 — the contract §18.5 has promised since 28.0 is now enforced by the `Tx`.** `database/reservation.go` (new), `database/transaction.go`, `database/transaction_test.go`, `database/pgx/reservation_integration_test.go` (new), `database/stdlib/reservation_integration_test.go` (new), plus `docs/PRD.md` for the one decision this sub-item owned. **Zero template edits, zero golden movement.** `connSem` is one capacity-1 channel on `Tx`: `Exec` holds it across the driver call, `Query` hands it to the result set and `txRows.Next()` gives it back **at exhaustion**, `txRow.Scan` gives it back at the end of the scan. The design's central claim held — `for rows.Next() { … }` then the next statement is *already* the right shape, so not one call site changed, in the runtime or in any of the twelve generated trees.

**The depth rule from 28.0 is what the acquire mode keys off, and the savepoint paths hold the reservation across their bookkeeping, not just their statement.** `Begin` and a savepoint-depth `Commit`/`Rollback` take the blocking acquire; only a root teardown uses the try-acquire and proceeds without it. Holding `connSem` across the push/pop is the acceptance criterion in its own right: released in between, two goroutines could exec their `SAVEPOINT`s in one order and push their names in the other, and the stack would disagree with the server's. That is gated by unwinding sixteen concurrently-opened frames and asserting the `RELEASE` order inverts the `SAVEPOINT` order exactly — which fails immediately when the release is moved before the bookkeeping.

**The `IsClosed()` question 28.1 carried forward is decided, and neither of the two candidates it named survived contact.** 28.1's early flip contradicts **§18.4's own wording** — "returns true after the transaction has been committed or rolled back" — and takes the wrong branch on all four reader surfaces, three of them **silently**: §18.6 fires a side effect for a transaction that has not committed (its critical invariant), `database.Conn` routes a concurrent read to the **pool** where it cannot see the transaction's own writes, `NewTransaction` opens a second root transaction instead of a savepoint, and `InTransaction` refuses a §9.6a lock-mode read. The reviewer's plain `tearingDown` drops a callback registered during a *succeeding* teardown. **The third option the task list named is what landed**: `closed` now means the teardown completed, `tearingDown` is the separate claim that makes a root teardown at-most-once, and **the callback list is snapshotted after the driver call**, in the same critical section that sets `closed` — so an `OnCommit` landing while the `COMMIT` is on the wire is still picked up. What remains is the gap between a hook's own `IsClosed()` and its `OnCommit`, two acquisitions no snapshot ordering can fuse, now a few instructions wide instead of a network round trip. §18.4, §18.5 and §18.6 all state it; §18.6 carries the argument, because §18.6's invariant is what decides it.

**A statement issued during the teardown window is rejected rather than sent, and that asymmetry is the shape of the decision.** `Exec`, `Query`, `QueryRow` and `Begin` gate on `closed || tearingDown`, so they keep exactly what 28.1's early flip bought on those paths — an immediate named error instead of a wait followed by a driver complaint — while `IsClosed()` keeps telling the truth to the four surfaces that read it.

**One of 28.1's tests deadlocked, and finding out why is a result rather than a chore.** `TestSavepointUnwindSurvivesAnEmptiedStack` drove its interleaving by re-entering a savepoint teardown from *inside* another one's statement; under the reservation that waits for a lock its own caller holds, so **the single-goroutine spelling of that race no longer exists**. The window itself is still reachable — `depth` is read *before* the acquire, to choose the acquire mode, so a goroutine already holding the reservation can pop the frame while this one waits — so the guard stays and the test was rebuilt around two goroutines, with `synctest.Wait` standing in for the unobservable "the second one is parked on the reservation". Deleting the guard still panics with `index out of range [-1]`. `TestStatementsDoNotHoldStructMutex` lost its `wantClosed` column for the reason above; that was the forward coupling the task list predicted.

**Six probes were confirmed failing-first against surgically reverted trees**, and the measurements are the argument: removing the reservation from `Tx.Query` reproduces the pgx data races; deleting `txRows.Next()`'s release hangs the savepoint-wait gate, which synctest reports as a stuck bubble rather than a timeout; snapshotting the callbacks *before* the driver call drops the window callback; restoring the early `closed` flip fails all three window assertions; releasing before `Begin`'s bookkeeping breaks the ordering gate; deleting the depth guard panics. Against real databases: the three-way fan-out inside `WithTx` runs at **0 races** on pgx and the connection survives it; a leaked result set meets `pgx commit: conn busy` in **0.02s** rather than stranding the connection; and all three bounded-wait probes — `ctx` deadline, `TxOptions.Timeout`, and §2.3's undrained two-row set on the same goroutine — fail at the 2s bound with the reservation's own named error and **never** `conn busy`. The same fan-out passes on MySQL and SQLite, and that half is honestly *not* failing-first: `database/sql` already serialized it internally, so what it asserts is that the reservation did not change that.

**The auto-review found seven things; five were fixed inside the sub-item and two are handed forward because 28.2 may not edit a template.** The substantive fix is a hole in §18.5's own rule, of exactly the class 28.0's decision was correcting: §18.5 justified blocking at savepoint depth with *"a savepoint statement runs while the enclosing `fn` is still live"*, which is **false for the one savepoint teardown `WithTransaction` issues on its own behalf** — by then its `fn` has returned, so a nested scope that leaks a result set and unwinds waits **for itself**, forever by default, and the root teardown is never reached either because the hung goroutine sits below it on the stack. The identical leak at the root returns in 0.02s. **The acquire mode was not changed, and that is the answer rather than a deferral**: `Commit`/`Rollback` take only a `ctx` and cannot tell that teardown from a consumer's own `Commit` racing another goroutine's read, where a try-acquire fails a generated `…WithRelated` with `conn busy` — FIX-221's defect returning. §18.5 now states the cost, why the exemption cannot be extended, and carries the missing failure-mode row. Also fixed: the caller rule had **no `QueryRow` analogue** (a `Row` has no `Close`, so discarding one unscanned is the same leak); §18.6's residual window was understated, since `cache.go.tmpl:1002` registers **two** callbacks behind one `IsClosed()` check and the gap therefore admits a *split* rather than an all-or-nothing drop; `acquireConn`'s error doubled the transaction name; and one `//nolint:wrapcheck` reason was simply wrong.

**Two findings are handed forward, and the second wants a decision.** `Raw` still returns `database.Rows` up through the hook chain, so a hook erroring after the terminal ran now leaks a reservation rather than only a result set — **28.5's scan callback removes it**. And `mysqlVersionCache.get` (`client.go.tmpl:107`) runs `database.Conn(ctx, …).QueryRow` inside a client-wide `sync.Once` — **now task-listed on 28.3**, which is already editing the guard the probe sits inside. Measuring it sharpened the finding: the probe is **always** inside a transaction, not sometimes, because `_lock_mode_guard.tmpl:33` reaches `get()` only after `database.InTransaction(ctx)` passes. The two failure modes have different causes — the **permanently poisoned cache** is this sub-item's `IsClosed()` decision (`InTransaction` now reports `true` during a teardown where the early flip made it `false`, so the guard no longer rejects before `get()` runs), while the **client-wide stall** is the reservation itself. The fix is one line and is a simplification: `SELECT VERSION()` is a server property, not transaction state, so the probe should read `c.querier` directly.

**What 28.2 handed forward:** **28.3** (`Stream`'s in-transaction guard + `AllowInTransaction`, carrying the `mysqlVersionCache.get` probe fix above) and **28.4** (remove FIX-221's `g.SetLimit(1)`), plus the `Raw` reservation leak booked on **28.5**. 28.3 is done — see Current Focus.

**28.1 (runtime preconditions) complete 2026-09-21 — the two things 28.2 rests on are in place.** `database/transaction.go`, `database/stdlib/stdlib.go`, `database/pgx/pgx.go` and their tests. Runtime module only, **zero golden movement**, no template touched. `Begin`, `Commit` and `Rollback` now snapshot the connection under `tx.mu`, release it across the `SAVEPOINT` / `RELEASE SAVEPOINT` / `ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT` / `COMMIT` / `ROLLBACK` statement, and re-take it only to record the bookkeeping — the shape `Exec`, `Query` and `QueryRow` already had. `database/stdlib`'s `rows.Next()` closes the underlying `sql.Rows` on exhaustion, so connection release coincides with `Next() == false` exactly as it already does on pgx.

**Both acceptance probes were confirmed failing-first, and one of them names the whole point of the change.** `TestStatementsDoNotHoldStructMutex` reaches back into the `Tx` through `IsClosed()` *and* `OnCommit()` from inside the driver call — the two acquisitions real generated code makes, via `database.Conn` at the head of every read and the cache layer's `dispatchRefresh`. Against the pre-change tree it deadlocks on five of its six cases; `plain exec` passes there, which is correct and is the point: `Exec` already had the right shape and the other five now match it. The §2.11 MySQL probe reproduces the design's table exactly — control passes before and after, multi fails before with `tx exec: driver: bad connection` **and** `stdlib rollback: invalid connection`, the poisoned-transaction pair.

**The untangle removes a guarantee `tx.mu` was providing incidentally, and dropping it would have been a silent regression.** Holding the mutex across `conn.Commit` is what made root teardown at-most-once: a second `Commit`, or a `Rollback` racing it, blocked for the round trip and then read `closed == true`. Release the mutex and both reach the driver — firing the `OnCommit` callbacks **twice**, so every deferred event publishes twice and every cache invalidation runs twice, and racing pgx's own unguarded `dbTx.closed`. **`closed` therefore flips *before* the driver call rather than after**, restored if the driver rejects the teardown so a failed `COMMIT` can still be rolled back. That is also the answer `IsClosed()` gave before — a concurrent caller blocked for the round trip and then observed `true` — so the wait is gone and the answer is not. This matters beyond 28.1: §18.5 says root teardown proceeds **without** the reservation, so after 28.2 the reservation will not serialize teardown either, and the claim is the only thing that does.

**A second incidental guarantee, and this one would have been a panic.** With the mutex held for the whole body, `tx.depth` could not change between the `RELEASE SAVEPOINT` and the bookkeeping. Without it, two concurrent savepoint scopes can leave `tx.depth == 0` at the re-lock, and `tx.callbacks[tx.depth-1]` then indexes `-1` — a panic in the consumer's process where the old code merely unwound the wrong frame. §18.5 carves concurrent scopes out as unsupported and explicitly declines to *detect* them, so the guard detects nothing: it restores the old failure mode and nothing more. One pre-existing data race was fixed in passing, in code this sub-item was restructuring anyway — root `Commit` read `tx.cancel` outside the lock where `Rollback` already snapshotted it.

**`Tx`'s doc comment was deliberately left promising less than §18.5 does.** It still says concurrent statements are unsafe and that two goroutines on one `txCtx` is undefined behavior, because that is still true at 28.1 — the reservation does not exist until 28.2, and **28.6** is the documentation sweep that makes the promise. The paragraph added here states only what 28.1 establishes: no `tx.mu` acquisition spans a round trip, and why.

**The auto-review found five things; four were fixed inside the sub-item.** A `t.Fatalf` called from a spawned goroutine (`guidelines/TESTING.md:396` forbids it outright); a test that would **panic the binary on a regression** rather than report it, by closing its signal channel unconditionally inside `commitFn`; restore-on-error comments claiming *"the caller may retry or roll back"*, which is true of `Tx` and **false of both shipped adapters** — with a test that asserted `Rollback() == nil` and passed only because the mock's nil `rollbackFn` returns nil, pinning mock behaviour rather than adapter behaviour; and a `Tx` doc paragraph that never mentioned the early `closed` flip. A fifth finding the review filed against 28.2 was closed here instead, because 28.1 introduced it: **`Begin` returned success for a savepoint scope the caller does not have** when a root teardown landed between its `closed` check and its bookkeeping push — two steps now that the mutex no longer spans the statement, one atomic step before. The second critical section re-checks `closed`, and `TestBeginRejectsATeardownRacingItsSavepoint` makes the interleaving deterministic by tearing the transaction down *from inside the `SAVEPOINT` statement*, which is only reachable because of this sub-item's own change.

**One finding is deliberately booked on 28.2 rather than fixed: what `IsClosed()` should report during a root teardown.** The early flip means a teardown *that then fails* reports `true` while the transaction is still open, so the cache layer's §18.6 hook shape (`cache.go.tmpl:1002`, `:1027`) invalidates immediately instead of deferring. The reviewer's proposed `tearingDown` alternative does not dominate it: on the far more common *succeeding* path it reports `false`, the cache calls `OnCommit`, and the callback lands **after** `Commit` already snapshotted `tx.callbacks[0]` — a silently dropped invalidation, indefinite staleness, where the early flip's failure mode is one wasted re-read. Neither recovers the pre-change behaviour, which was correct for both outcomes only because it **blocked** — and "root teardown never waits" is §18.5's own recorded decision, so this is a consequence of the phase's design rather than a defect 28.1 chose. 28.2 owns that acquire and has a third option available (snapshot the callbacks after the driver call); whichever it takes, §18.5 or §18.6 must then say so, because today neither does.

**28.1 verified 2026-09-22.** Both acceptance probes were re-confirmed failing-first by an independent pass — `transaction.go` reverted to HEAD deadlocks five of the gate's six cases, and `stdlib.go` reverted against a live MySQL 8.0 container reproduces the poisoned-transaction pair — and `make check`, `go test -race ./database/...` and `make check-examples` (all twelve trees) are green on the pinned toolchain. The pass found one gap and closed it inside the sub-item: the savepoint depth guard had **no regression gate**, so deleting it passed the whole suite. It also corrected one claim in the record above — the stdlib drain-close does not swallow a driver close error; Go 1.27.1's `Rows.close` ends `rs.lasterr = rs.lasterrOrErrLocked(err)`, so the error is relocated from `Close()` to `Err()`, which is where every site that checks it already looks. Two amendments went into **28.2**'s `IsClosed()` task rather than a FIX entry: the affected consumer list is **four** surfaces, not one, and the failed-COMMIT consequence is §18.6's critical invariant verbatim.

**What 28.1 handed forward:** **28.2** (the reservation itself), carrying the `IsClosed()`-during-teardown decision above on its task list — settled there, see the 28.2 entry.

**28.0 (Phase 28 PRD sync) complete 2026-09-21 — the transaction connection-lock contract is now normative.** `docs/PRD.md` only, +157/−33, no code and no golden movement. §18.5 is the normative home and was rewritten rather than supplemented: its two load-bearing claims — *"the caller is what enforces that"* and §20.5's *"a `txCtx` used from two goroutines at once is undefined behavior"* — were **false** under the design and are now replaced by a per-method reservation table, release-on-drain, and the single rule that remains the caller's (*drain or close a result set before issuing the next statement on that `txCtx`*), stated identically in §18.5, §20.2 and §9.4a.

**The `Begin` question the phase breakdown flagged was settled by finding a generated path it broke.** `docs/design/archive/TX_CONNECTION_LOCK.md` §3.4 routes `Begin`, `Commit` **and** `Rollback` through the non-blocking teardown acquire, justified by *"`fn` has already returned"* — which is true only at the **root**. `Begin` and a savepoint-depth `Commit`/`Rollback` run while the enclosing `fn` is live, and every `…WithRelated` mutation opens a savepoint (`nested.go.tmpl:268`, `:333`, `:396`), so a try-acquire would have left a nested mutation failing with `conn busy` against a concurrent read — a first-class generated path, not an edge. **Decision: depth sets the acquire mode, not verb.** Savepoint statements block; only root teardown proceeds without the reservation. That strictly dominates §3.4's blanket rule, and it makes 28.1's `tx.mu` untangle a **hard precondition** rather than the optional tidy-up §5.2 step 1 calls it — a blocking acquire while holding `tx.mu` is a lock-order inversion against every `IsClosed()` on the read path.

**Working that decision through surfaced a limit the design doc does not carry, now a §18.5 carve-out.** The reservation serializes **statements**, not savepoint **scopes**. `Tx` tracks savepoint names as a stack, so two goroutines calling `WithTx` — or any `…WithRelated` mutation — on the same `txCtx` *at the same time* interleave their pushes, and the release one issues can name the savepoint the other opened. No statement-level lock fixes that; each individual statement is correctly serialized and it is the scopes that overlap. Documented rather than detected, since detecting it needs the goroutine identity §6.1 declined to track. Sequential nesting is unaffected. The per-dialect `RELEASE SAVEPOINT` cascade semantics were deliberately **not** probed — the bookkeeping mismatch is established from this repo's own code, and the carve-out is conservative under any dialect answer.

**`AllowInTransaction` emits unconditionally**, like `LockMode` and `SkipEvents`. The alternative — gating it on the package emitting at least one `Stream` — would make `CallOptions`'s shape depend on the operations mask, so a consumer who turns `operations.stream` off would find unrelated code stop compiling. The tenancy fields are gated because tenancy is a project-wide mode a package either has or does not; an operations toggle is not. Golden churn is not a counter-argument in an unreleased project. The manifest's `conventions.call_options.fields` carries it for the same reason it carries `LockMode`.

**The auto-review found eleven sites the first pass missed, and all eleven were fixed inside the sub-item.** The headline one is the acceptance criterion's own: **§9.4a never actually stated the drain-or-close rule** — it stated the opt-in's obligation and left the reader to infer the two were the same sentence. Beside it, the "every stale claim is caught" assertion was falsified in six more places, two of which mattered beyond tidiness: §13.2's own intro still said "serially inside a transaction — see step 4 below", contradicting the step 4 this sub-item had just reverted; and §8's Transaction Tests section said the `Tx` "does not make concurrent *statements* safe, **and no test may assert that it does**" — which forbade the tests 28.2 and 28.4 require. Both are fixed, the second by asserting the serialization and carving out the two shapes that genuinely may not be asserted. The rest: two more copies of the reverted §13.2 claim (§9.4's method table, §9.8.3), three generated doc-comment samples in §9.8 making claims *about* §13.2 that §13.2 no longer makes, §18.5's own failure-mode table saying "teardown never waits" unqualified where the depth rule three paragraphs below says root-only, §9.4a's hook-integration paragraph missing that a refused `Stream` fires **no** hooks including global authorization, §19.1's `QueryFunc` entry omitting the `Querier` parameter the real signature carries, and the last copy of `Raw`'s old signature in `IMPLEMENTATION_ORDER.md`. The reviewer independently verified and endorsed both departures from the design doc, and confirmed §9.4a is implementable by 28.3 — with one gap now on 28.3's task list: **§9.4a does not pin `AllowInTransaction`'s position in the struct**, which moves goldens either way.

**Two observations logged for later sub-items rather than fixed here** (28.0 is no-code by construction): `manifest/builder.go:334`'s `call_options.fields` list names a `Tx` field the generated `CallOptions` does not have and omits `FieldOptions` plus both tenancy fields — pre-existing drift that **28.3** will be touching that list anyway; and §9.8's `RETURNING`-then-re-read claim, cited in §18.5's two-locks argument, was carried forward from the previous wording rather than re-verified against HEAD.


**27.8 (Go `CreateWithRelated`, Ticket F) complete 2026-09-18 — the first emitter has landed.** `cmd/sqlgen/gen/context_nested.go` resolves §9.9.4's eligibility once for both the emitter and `ValidateNestedWriteEligibility`, and `templates/table/nested.go.tmpl` renders the wrapper input, the per-edge create-side verb block, the nested child input and one executor per edge. `cmd/sqlgen/testdata/examples/graphql` is the only example that turns the feature on, and it is the right one: all four §9.9.1 write shapes, a polymorphic edge, a self-referential tenanted edge and four `filter:` negatives live in that one schema. Seven parents get a `CreateWithRelated`; `profiles` and `user_credentials` get none, which is the belongs-to acceptance criterion.

**The first run of the emitter silently dropped every config-declared edge, and the cause was a lookup key the whole codebase shares.** `configRelationshipToContext` carries a config `table: documents` unsplit — `TargetSchema` stays empty — while every table context is keyed `public.documents`. `assets.Attachments`, `assets.Invoices`, `assets.PrimaryDocument` and `workspace_notes.Children` all came back "target not in the generated set" and were omitted with no signal, because an auto-included edge that fails a rule is *supposed* to be silent. The fix generalizes 27.7's junction hazard rather than patching the target lookup: one `tableIndex` resolves in three steps — the schema the edge names, then the parent's own schema, then the bare name **only when it is unambiguous across the generated set** — and is used for both the relationship target and the M2M junction. That closes the multi-schema collision 27.8 inherited on its ticket and the unqualified-config-target miss in one mechanism.

**D18(c) had no reachable fixture, and finding that out changed the schema.** All three `assets` discriminator edges run over `documents.entity_id`, which is NOT NULL — so E8 leaves them `create`-only and their visibility read is never emitted. The D18(c) task would have shipped with a template branch nothing exercises. `workspace_notes.DraftChildren` (`fk: parent_id`, `discriminator: {column: kind, value: draft}`) is the only shape that closes it: polymorphic **and** over a nullable FK. It is also self-referential and on a tenanted table, so one added edge carries D18(a), D18(b), D18(c), E12 and §29.10's tenant propagation. **Its failing-first run is the argument for the rule**: with the predicate removed the connect returns `err = nil` and relinks a `published` note into an edge that can never read it back — a silent mislink, not a wrong error.

**Four narrowings the PRD did not state were implemented fail-closed, and the user ruled on all four on 2026-09-18: keep them and amend the spec.** (1) **Every** M2M verb needs the junction's `upsert_many`, not just E9's two — §9.9.6's link step is a batched upsert on the junction and it runs for `create` as much as for `connect`; **E9 amended**. (2) An M2M edge also needs a PK-covering conflict-target constant on its junction, for the same reason E10 needs one on the parent; **§9.9.4 gains E13** and the emitter's rule id moved to match. (3) A target whose PK is in the `comparator.Opaque` family (§11.2) carries no `connect` — the visibility read buckets on that key and the adoption filters on it, and none of those four Go types is a valid map key; **E8 amended**, including that this half is *reported* rather than dropped silently. (4) **Found by the auto-review, and the only fail-*open* one**: an M2M edge with a `discriminator:` set the predicate on the `connect` visibility read and nowhere on the `create` arm, so a nested create inserted a target the edge's own loader can never return and then linked it — the silent mislink D18(c) closes one arm over. §9.9.5 ("an M2M `create` takes the target's input unchanged") and D18(a) ("a nested create SETs the discriminator") cannot both hold on that shape. The write surface is refused under **E2**, the read surface is untouched, and **§9.9.5, §13.4.1 and §26.12 are amended** alongside — §13.4.1 needed it most, since framing `discriminator:` as the invertible alternative to `filter:` implied it makes any polymorphic edge writable.

**A verb the emitter cannot render is now reported rather than dropped.** That is a different fact from a verb the §4.6 `verbs` mask turned off: the mask is the consumer asking for a narrower surface and is silent by design, while an unrenderable verb is a generator limitation invisible from the config. `nestedIneligible` gained a `verb` field — a note naming none takes the edge, a note naming one leaves the edge with the verbs that work — and the lint reports it for an explicitly-listed edge. The review also read `nestedNullFilterExpr`'s three-spelling family test as a live silent drop; measured against `applyConfigDeclaredFKs`, which stamps a synthetic `FKReference` on every config-declared `fk:`, no schema reaches the other members. The widening landed anyway as the correct rule, labelled defensive.

**`allow_reparent` was implemented here rather than left inert, and §9.9.6 gained what the flag's name does not imply.** The key landed in 27.0a and §9.9.6 already made it normative; a config key that validates and does nothing is the accept-and-drop shape the separate input types exist to prevent. Two consequences were undocumented and are now written down: the middle outcome disappears, so `connect` is two-way and `ErrAlreadyRelated` is unreachable (§9.9.8's table says so), and the post-`UPDATE` shortfall reports `ErrNotFound` — with no `fk IS NULL` term the only remaining cause is a row that stopped being visible. No example sets it.

**The §9.9.5 edge ordering was wrong in 27.7 and is corrected.** `nestedCandidateEdges` sorted by the declared `Name`, and an auto-detected edge carries the pluralized snake form (`documents`) where a config-declared one carries what the consumer wrote (`Attachments`) — so ASCII-ordering the two spellings groups by origin, and `assets` emitted its M2M edge last. `FieldName` is the spelling §9.9.8 attributes errors with and §26.5.5 puts in `extensions.path`, so sorting on it makes the three observable orderings agree.

**The query-count pin asserts §9.9.7's invariant rather than a total.** The same three-edge call runs with 2 children and again with 20 and the counts must be equal; only a per-row read breaks that, and a fixed expected total would move on any unrelated change to an inner client. A loose ceiling sits beside it to catch a regression that adds one round-trip per verb, which would keep the two runs equal.

**Booked on 27.9 by the user's direction.** The manifest (§30) enumerates every mutation method with its `SQLBodies`, and `CreateWithRelated` is absent — an agent reading the manifest cannot discover the method. The entry needs a shape decision the existing one does not fit: a nested method has no single SQL body, being a composition of its inner calls' statements. 27.9 adds two more with the same gap, so **all three take one decision and one landing there**; it is on 27.9's task list.

**`/verify 27.8` (2026-09-18) returned 7/8 PRD PASS, 8/8 required tests and 6/6 prior fixes confirmed.** Eight of its nine findings were fixed inside the sub-item — three code (`nestedEdgeImports` under-collecting on M2M under `file_per_table`, a *false* E1 diagnostic for a schema-qualified config `table:`, and an E8 rule id cited on an M2M edge), three PRD-amendment defects this sub-item's own edits created, and two stale censuses annotated rather than rewritten. A guideline violation the parent found is fixed alongside: six resolvers returned `*nestedIneligible` as a second return value, which is literally the `(*T, *ConcreteError)` typed-nil shape `guidelines/GO.md` forbids — the type is a `fmt.Stringer` now, so the conversion is unrepresentable.

**The coverage finding is 27.7's lesson recurring for the third time.** `BuildSharedTypesContext` gained a `hasNested` parameter that every unit call site passed `false`, so both new package helpers were exercised by an E2E golden alone — and only by the *tenanted* one, since `graphql` is the single example that enables the feature, leaving `nestedChildOptions`'s non-tenanted body rendered by nothing. `TestNestedSharedHelpers_BodiesVaryWithTenancy` now covers all three package shapes.

**FIX-220 (`blocking`) is Resolved (2026-09-18), and its fix outgrew the §29.6 bound it was filed for.** §29.10 says nested children inherit the §29.6 ctx-cached tenant; each generated mutation was stashing the resolved tenant on its *own* local ctx, which never escaped back to the transaction closure, so a two-edge nested create invoked the consumer's `TenantResolver` eight times where §29.6 permits one. The hoist now lives in `nested.go.tmpl`, gated on `NeedsTenantResolver` — "some client this method reaches resolves a tenant", not "an edge target is tenanted", since the parent's own terminal `Get` re-read resolves too. Fixing it required a second correction in `tenancy/`: the ctx slot had been caching the generated helper's `apply` bool, which is the *table's* reading of the resolver's answer and folds in that table's `tenancy.required`. Keyed on `T` alone, that slot is shared by every entity client in the package, so whichever table resolved first imposed its `required` on every table downstream — a `required:false` table resolving to the zero value handed `apply=false` to a `required:true` table, which then skipped the `tenancy.ErrMissing` it owed and **fell open**. `WithResolvedTenant` / `CachedTenant` now store the raw answer and each `resolveTenant` re-applies its own branch.

**A second `/verify 27.8` after FIX-220 landed found one behavioural defect the first pass missed, fixed inside the sub-item.** A **repeated id in a `connect` list reported `ErrAlreadyRelated` against a row with no other parent** — and with no id attached, the shortfall branch not being id-scoped. The adoption verifies itself by comparing the `UPDATE`'s row count against the queued id count, and the `UPDATE` matches the row once however many times it was named; `comparator.parseIn` does not dedupe either. That is the *"fewer rows updated than requested → conflict"* collapse §9.9.6 forbids, arriving through the caller's input rather than through the guard. The M2M link step had always deduped; the O2M / has-one arm had not. `connect` is a set operation, so the adoption now dedupes **after** the three-way split — a repeated orphan id is one adoption, a repeated *parented* id still conflicts and still names itself. Confirmed failing-first against a real PostgreSQL container. Four doc/label findings from the same pass were fixed alongside: an unreachable M2M discriminator arm on the visibility read (dead since E2 began refusing such edges, and fail-open on the create arm the day E2 is lifted), `NestedMutationError.Verb`'s doc omitting `"link"`, a stale `TargetTenanted` field reference, and the "no verb survives" note hard-coding rule id **E8** where the causes compose — it cites §9.9.3's matrix now. Two reviewer observations were **not** fixed, being pre-existing gaps rather than 27.8 defects: per-edge `Imports` is rendered by no `file_per_table` golden, and M2M generated-PK derivation is uncovered on non-`RETURNING` dialects.

**Next:** **27.9a** (nested mutations on MySQL, the non-`RETURNING` write path) — **unblocked** by 27.9. Then **27.10** (the GraphQL projection, Ticket H), which needs 27.8 and 27.9 and now has both.

**27.9 (Go `UpdateWithRelated` + `UpsertWithRelated`, Ticket G) complete 2026-09-18.** The §9.9.3 matrix is whole: `disconnect` and `clear` are the two verbs that only exist once the parent does, and all seven `graphql` parents carry the trio. One executor per edge serves all three families — its parameter widened from the create-side block to the update-side one, with `CreateWithRelated` lifting its own block at the call, which is sound precisely because `Disconnect` and `Clear` are absent from the create-side *type* (D4), so the widening cannot smuggle a verb in. The family gate moved off `Operations` and onto `NestedContext.Emit{Create,Update,Upsert}`: 27.8 skipped the whole surface when `create_with_related` was off, which was right with one family and would have taken `UpdateWithRelated` with it once there were three. **E10's second clause** lands there too — `UpsertWithRelated` takes a `<Parent>ConflictTarget` argument, and a table whose uniqueness is app-enforced through `primary_key.columns` emits no such constant, so the upsert family alone is dropped.

**D18 is extended to the unlink verbs, and §9.9.6 / §13.4.1 are amended to say so (D18(d)).** `clear` unlinks every row *on this edge*, and a polymorphic edge is `fk = parent AND <disc> = <value>`. Without the second term a `clear` on `workspace_notes.DraftChildren` unlinks the `published` children too — rows the caller never named, reported as success. `Children` and `DraftChildren` share `parent_id`, so the fixture reaches it, and the failing-first run detaches the published child under both `clear` and `disconnect`. The same predicate now also scopes the **`connect` adoption UPDATE**, closing the hazard 27.8 recorded and did not act on: the visibility read carried it and the UPDATE did not, so a target whose discriminator changed between the two was adopted into an edge that can never read it back. §9.9.6's pseudocode predated discriminator edges being write-eligible; by the user's ruling of 2026-09-18 — the same shape as 27.8's four narrowings — the execution-order block now marks the term on all four statements that carry it, and §13.4.1's write-side ownership list goes from three rules to four, the fourth stated as rule 3 inverted: rule 3 stops a row entering an edge that cannot read it, rule 4 stops rows *leaving* an edge that was never writing to them. **§9.9.4 was corrected alongside**, in two places this sub-item's own work made wrong: E8 scoped the map-keyable-PK requirement to `connect` where `disconnect` needs it too, and said nothing about the target's `update_where`, which all three unlink-or-adopt verbs route through; and the preamble's census of what gets *reported* still read "E8's primary-key clause is the only one today".

**The auto-review found three code defects, all fixed inside the sub-item, and the first would not have compiled.** A `disconnect` on a `comparator.Opaque`-keyed target emitted `map[[]byte]bool`: D13's verb-conflict check buckets the named ids in a set, and the map-key guard was scoped to the self-referential E12 case alone. The fixture reaches it (`blobs`), and **this sub-item's own census test asserted the bug** — `renderNested` parses the emitted Go without type-checking it, so nothing in the repo ever compiled that shape. The guard is unconditional now, and an invariant test asserts it over every fixture edge against the resolver's own predicate rather than restating §11.2's family. Second, `update_where: false` on a target was rejecting the *whole edge* — a hard build error for any allowlisted edge — where E4 scopes to `create` / `create_many` and an `operations` entry is a config-visible fact §9.9.4 makes silent; it narrows the verb set silently now. Third, the `connect` adoption could still lose its D18(d) term where the unlink half refused, because the read's predicate is raw SQL on the column and the UPDATE's a filter member; the adoption refuses on the same condition now.

**E11 is implemented for the first time** — 27.8 had no verb it could apply to. A junction with a soft-delete column loses `disconnect` and `clear` and keeps `create` and `connect`; no example junction has the column, so `user_tags` was added to the gen unit fixture rather than shipping a rule no test reaches. An M2M `disconnect` additionally needs the junction keyed on exactly its two FKs, since `HardDeleteMany` takes the junction's own key.

**The query-count pin 27.8 shipped was vacuous, and 27.9's Q9 pin is what found it.** `countingQuerier` wraps a `database.Querier`, and `database.Conn` hands a transaction body the `*database.Tx` — which holds the driver connection the shim delegated to when it answered `Begin`. Every statement between BEGIN and COMMIT bypassed it, so `TestCreateWithRelated_QueryCountDoesNotScaleWithChildren` was comparing 1 against 1. A pgx `QueryTracer` on its own pool sits below that boundary; both pins use it now, and the 27.8 one gained a floor so it cannot go vacuous again.

**FIX-221 (`blocking`) was found here and deliberately not fixed here.** `loadRelationships` fans its per-edge reads across an unbounded errgroup; inside a transaction they all run on one driver connection, so selecting **two or more** relationships fails with pgx `conn busy` — and the failed rollback then destroys the transaction. Measured pre-existing at HEAD on two probes: 27.8's shipped `CreateWithRelated` with three relationships selected, and a plain `Get` with the same three inside a caller `WithTx`. It bites the nested surface hardest, because §9.9.6 step 3 places the terminal re-read inside the method's own transaction, so §9.9.5's "select a relationship and get it back" fails **always** for a parent with two selected edges. The fix lands in `get.go.tmpl` and moves every example's `models_gen.go`, which is outside this sub-item — **user direction 2026-09-18: file it, keep 27.9 scoped**. The multi-edge terminal re-read is therefore covered by nothing, on either the create or the update side, until FIX-221 is resolved. **Resolved 2026-09-19**, outside 27.9 as planned: the loader now bounds its errgroup to one worker inside a transaction (`get.go.tmpl`), the PRD's false `Tx`-concurrency contract (§18.5/§20.5) was corrected alongside it, and the multi-edge terminal re-read gained coverage on both the create and the update side in `relationship_tx_fanout_test.go`.

**27.9a added 2026-09-18 by the user's direction — nested mutations on MySQL, the non-`RETURNING` write path.** PostgreSQL and SQLite both support `RETURNING`, so on both of them every nested inner call learns which rows it wrote from the statement that wrote them. MySQL does not, and compensates with two mechanisms the other dialects never take: `collectAffectedIDs` **pre-SELECTs** matching rows before an `UpdateWhere`, and `multiInsertAndResolveIDs` **derives** a batch's generated keys as `firstID + i`. The nested executor rests on both — the adoption's shortfall check counts what `UpdateWhere` returned, the M2M link step keys junction rows on what `CreateMany` returned — so MySQL is the one dialect where 27.8's and 27.9's correctness arguments hold against a different mechanism than the one they were written and tested against. The highest-consequence case is the key derivation: an off-by-one links the parent to the **wrong target row** and reports success, with the right row count. `graphql` is PostgreSQL and is the only example that enables the feature, so turning it on in the `mysql` example — plus one nullable-FK edge, since every MySQL fixture FK is `NOT NULL` and **E8** would otherwise leave every O2M edge `create`-only — is part of the sub-item rather than a precondition. It carries the full lifecycle: `CreateWithRelated` → `UpdateWithRelated` → `UpsertWithRelated` → `DeleteMany` in one caller transaction, asserted between steps. **Blocked by 27.9.**

---

**27.6 (`OpUpsertMany`'s three consumer arms) complete 2026-09-17, verified 2026-09-18, closing FIX-219.** Ticket **Dc**: cache invalidation joins §27.7's "Invalidate many" arm (not `OpCreateMany`'s write-through arm), `mapOpToAction` maps the op to the existing `event.Upsert` so no new wire action is minted, and the §32.3 redaction switch gains a per-row arm. `EventTableContext.HasCreateMany` (bool) became `BatchCreateOps` (`[]string`) — the ops whose §28.6 payload is a `[]*Create<T>Input` indexed per row — so `OpCreateMany` and `OpUpsertMany` share one arm composed from a list rather than from two booleans. Golden movement: 43 event-hook tables across six example trees, plus every `cache_gen.go` in the repo — five trees, in both `models/` and `expected/`.

**All seven pins were verified failing-first against the pre-fix generated code, each for its own reason.** Not "observed to pass afterwards": the fixed `event_hooks_gen.go` / `cache_gen.go` were swapped for their HEAD versions and the suite re-run. `ActionIsUpsert` reported `only 0 events delivered` (the `event.Upsert` filter never matched the minted `"upsert_many"`), both per-row pins reported `Input type = []*models.CreateProductInput` (the whole batch slice), the redacted pin reported `Input type = <nil>` (the §32.3 fail-closed default), and the cache pin reported `want 1 invalidate_many, got 0` (the bare `return nil`).

**`/verify 27.6` (2026-09-18) found the five `cache_gen.go` goldens hand-wrapped rather than regenerated — and no local gate catches that shape.** The template emits the widened case list on one line; both committed copies carried it wrapped across two. They agreed with each other, so the `expected/` ↔ `models/` check passed; both compiled and linted, so `make check-examples` passed; and `make check` runs `go test -short`, which `TestE2EGoldenFiles` skips. Only CI's non-short `cmd/sqlgen` job compares generated output against `expected/`, so this would have failed on push. Fixed by `make update-golden-e2e` (same file set, no others). The gate list in `/implement` Step 5 and `/verify` Step 7 names `make check` + `make check-examples` and runs neither E2E golden comparison — a hand-edit applied consistently to both copies passes every local gate.

**The auto-review returned 5/5 PRD and 0 blocking, and its three findings were closed rather than logged.** It re-walked the index-alignment audit independently across every branch of `upsert_many.go.tmpl` — composite PK, app / caller, `db` with read-back, non-covering target, the `c.batchSize` loop, both arms of the `$captureTenant` fork — confirming `len(m.Input) == len(m.AffectedPKs)` with matching order throughout. Two findings were **tenanted `UpsertMany` being generated but never exercised anywhere**, and **the `$mayReadBack` + `$captureTenant` branch appearing in no committed golden**. The reviewer scoped both to 27.5; the first is really 27.6's, because the cache arm added here is what first routes `OpUpsertMany` into `invalidateAffectedTenanted`, whose misalignment modes are not all loud: a **short** carrier trips `invalidateTenantedFallback`, which pattern-wipes and emits the cache error signal, but a **same-length** one is silent — a `nil` entry skips that row's eviction and a wrong-tenant entry evicts another tenant's key while the real one stays stale. `tenancy`'s `products` is exactly the shape both need (tenant **not** in the PK, `AUTOINCREMENT` key, a conflict target that does not cover it), so one new file — `tenancy/tests/upsert_many_tenancy_test.go` — closes both, and its two pins are failing-first as well. The third finding was a literal-text gap in 27.6's own Tests Required: the dedupe case existed only on the non-redacting table, so the redacted pin now uses a deduping batch too.

**The sub-item's own spec was wrong in two places, and it was corrected before any code was written.** `/verify 27.5` had recorded FIX-219 with a suggested fix that the task list then contradicted, so the entry was verified against the shipped code first — by measurement on the `events` example, per the probe-before-deciding rule.

1. **The EQ5 bullet argued against the correct fix and was obsolete.** It held that the per-row arm "cannot be a copy of `OpCreateMany`'s" because `DO NOTHING` + `RETURNING` returns only inserted rows, leaving `AffectedPKs` shorter than the input slice. **27.5 resolved EQ5 the other way**: `AffectedPKs` comes from the inputs, or from `resolveUpsertManyRows` (exactly one key per deduped value row, error otherwise), never from `RETURNING` (**A19**), and the terminal republishes `m.Input = rowInputs`, the deduped slice — a guarantee commit `ad575c5` wrote into PRD §28.9. Measured across all three branches: 3 inputs deduping to 2 rows → 2 events with `slice[i]` the input that wrote `AffectedPKs[i]`; a mixed batch of 2 conflicting + 1 new → 3 aligned events; a junction (`DO NOTHING`, empty `updateColumns`) builds `rowPKs` one-per-input with no read-back at all. The arm **is** an `OpCreateMany`-shaped index arm. Had the stale bullet been followed, 27.6 would have built a PK-to-input correlation mechanism for a problem 27.5 already closed. The bullet is corrected; the design docs' EQ5 / A15 / A19 rows are annotated rather than rewritten, being a historical record.
2. **The nil payload was scoped too narrowly.** FIX-219 described it as hitting a fail-closed table "that does not redact a *create* field". It is **every** fail-closed table: one that *does* redact a create field gets `case hook.OpCreate, hook.OpUpsert:`, which does not name `OpUpsertMany` either. Measured — `accounts` redacts create, update **and** increment inputs and still published nil on all three fanned-out events.

**One gate widened beyond what FIX-219 listed.** `batchCreateInputs` and its type assertion were gated on `HasCreateMany` alone. No `operations` preset produces `create_many: false, upsert_many: true` and no validation rule forbids the pair, but a per-table override reaches it — and under the old gate that config emits a package referencing an undeclared variable. Now gated on the union, pinned by `TestEventHooks_upsertManyWithoutCreateMany_stillDeclaresBatchLocals`.

**One pre-existing pin updated, not weakened.** `TestCacheTemplate_whereOpsShareKeyBasedArm` matches the invalidation arm's exact text, so adding `OpUpsertMany` to the `*Many` group broke it. The expected string now carries the new member and still asserts the same invariant — the `*Where` ops stay folded into the key-based arm rather than splitting off.

**PRD §28.6 gained the `UpsertMany` row it never had.** The shape table stopped after the `*Where` family, leaving §28.9's per-row prose with no normative row behind it. The new row also records that the indexed slice is the **deduped** one, not the caller's — the `CreateMany` / `UpdateMany` sentence says "the caller's input slice", which is not true of `UpsertMany`.

**27.5 (`UpsertMany`) complete 2026-09-17.** Ticket **Db**: a new `table/upsert-many` template on all three dialects, `UpsertMany(ctx, inputs, target, opts…)` mirroring `Upsert`'s signature over the same `<Entity>ConflictTarget` enum. `gen.ResolvedOperations` grows `UpsertMany`, the manifest advertises the method, and `hook.OpUpsertMany` lands (moved forward from 27.6 — see below). `make check` clean (37 packages); `make check-examples` green across all 12 example modules. **Golden movement across every tree, as forecast** — 269 files: `shared_types_gen.go` for two new helpers, the per-table files for the method and its interface entry, and the manifest artifacts.

**`/verify 27.5` found a key-sourcing defect in the sub-item's own code, and it was fixed inside the sub-item rather than carried (FIX-218, Resolved 2026-09-17).** The template decided where the written rows' keys come from using the table's PK **strategy**, where the governing fact is the **runtime conflict target**. Composite or `app` / `caller` strategy is necessary but not sufficient: the target must also cover every primary-key column. The key column is excluded from the update half of the clause — a row's identity is not a mutable attribute — so a row conflicting on some *other* unique constraint keeps the key it already had, and the input's key names a row that does not exist: a phantom key on `AffectedPKs` (the cache eviction key and `Event.PK`), `nil` tenants on a tenanted table, and a terminal `GetMany` that silently returns short. **The single-row `Upsert` never had it** — `upsert.go.tmpl:6-11` resolves through `RETURNING` on every RETURNING dialect *regardless of strategy*, and the batched form kept only the `ne .PKStrategy "db"` half of that condition.

**Measured, not argued, and the whole round of green suites is why it needed measuring.** Reinstating the pre-fix decision in place, everything else held constant, makes the new pin report `UpsertMany returned 1 entities, want 2` — the conflicting row dropped from the result. The fix moves the choice to call time: `ConflictTargetContext.CoversPK` is computed once in `buildConflictTargets`, and the template emits an `<entity>ConflictCoversPK` table the method reads per call, with `resolveUpsertManyRows` generalized to return composite keys. **The branch is emitted only where it can be taken** — the *set* of targets is fixed at generation time even though the choice among them is not — so every junction table and every caller-known-key table in all 12 example trees keeps the one-statement fast path. That is also why nothing caught it: no example tree gained a `pkFromInput` line, meaning no shipped example was ever reaching the defect. `counters` in the postgres example gained a nullable `slot TEXT UNIQUE` to make the shape reachable at runtime for the first time. PRD §9.2, §9.5 decision 3, §9.7 and §25.1 all stated the strategy-only rule and were amended; `TestUpsertManyTemplate_pkFromInput` had pinned the broken behavior and now asserts both directions.

**The acceptance criterion could not have caught it, which is the transferable lesson.** "`AffectedPKs` length equals the deduped input length" was true throughout: the input-sourced slice was always the right *length*, only its *values* were phantom. The criterion now reads "…and every key in it names a row that exists", and the pin asserts the returned entities carry the **seeded** key rather than the one the input supplied.

**The three measured decisions, and what each turns on.** (1) Inputs are deduped by conflict target before the first statement, last occurrence wins — PostgreSQL alone rejects an `ON CONFLICT … DO UPDATE` that would affect one row twice, so without the dedupe the operation is a PostgreSQL-only 500 on every table but a pure link table. A row whose conflict key is not definite and non-NULL is exempt, because a NULL never matches under a unique index and collapsing two such rows would drop one the caller asked for. (2) The `[]*T` return is one entity per deduped input on every dialect: entities come from a re-fetch by key, never from `RETURNING`, which omits every `DO NOTHING` row. (3) Keys are never read out of the statement — caller-known where the input carries them **and the conflict target covers the primary key**, read back by the conflict target otherwise. The second condition is FIX-218's correction; the original statement of this decision omitted it.

**Decision (3) was the fork, and the user took the wider branch on 2026-09-17.** The alternative was to gate emission on a caller-known key, which is what the design doc's §6.3 offers. It would have removed `UpsertMany` from most non-junction tables — `id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid()` resolves to the `db` strategy — leaving the batched form available on strictly fewer tables than the single-row `Upsert` already is, and gutting **D5**'s "independently useful" justification. The read-back is the batched form of FIX-196's `resolveUpsertConflictRow`, uniform across dialects, and a key-resolution read rather than a write, so §25.1's write bound is unchanged; PRD §9.7 now says so explicitly.

**The §6.3 measurement reproduces exactly, eleven weeks on.** The MySQL pin logs `LAST_INSERT_ID=2, seeded=1, actual ids=map[RawLII-a:1 RawLII-b:2 RawLII-c:3]` — the companion's 2026-09-11 numbers. `firstID + i` would name 2, 3, 4: wrong for all three rows. The test asserts the claim rather than the literal ids, so it stays a pin rather than a version snapshot.

**FIX-219 was 27.6's, deliberately, and 27.6 closed it (2026-09-17).** `/verify 27.5` found a **fourth** `OpUpsertMany` consumer site nobody had listed — `passThroughOps` (`context_event.go:270-281`) — and its two gates fail in opposite directions: on a fail-closed table that does not redact a *create* field, `UpsertMany` publishes `Event.Input: nil` where §28.6 mandates a per-row `*Create<T>Input` (live today at `graphql/models/event_hooks_gen.go:328`); on a non-fail-closed table the default publishes the whole deduped slice to every fanned-out event. It was **not** fixed inside 27.5 because adding `OpUpsertMany` to `passThroughOps` would trade one wrong shape for the other — the only correct fix is 27.6's own per-row arm, which must be emitted **unconditionally** with redaction nested inside (the `case hook.OpCreateMany:` shape), not wrapped in `{{- if .EmitRedactCreate }}` the way `case hook.OpCreate, hook.OpUpsert:` is. Both points, and the missing `UpsertMany` row in PRD §28.6's shape table, are now written into 27.6's tasks and acceptance criteria.

**`hook.OpUpsertMany` moved from 27.6 to 27.5.** The template cannot build a `MutationContext` without an op, and reusing `hook.OpUpsert` would hand a whole input slice to three arms written for a single row — including §32.3's redaction arm, the un-redacted-payload class FIX-207 exists to close. PRD §21.2 already declared the constant verbatim, and 27.6's own task text assumes it exists ("without it the op falls out of the switch to a bare `return nil`"). 27.6 keeps its real content — cache invalidation, `mapOpToAction`, the redaction arm — and its three pins are genuinely failing-first against this state.

**One hazard found next door, measured, not fixed here.** `CreateMany` called with zero inputs writes nothing and then re-fetches on a key filter built from an empty set — and every generated comparator treats an empty `In` as *no condition* (`comparator/id.go:29`), while the call passes `Limit: new(0)`. Probed on the postgres example: `CreateMany(ctx, nil)` issues `SELECT "created_at", "description", "id", "name" FROM "public"."categories"` with no `WHERE` and returns every row in the table. `UpsertMany` short-circuits on an empty input for exactly this reason. Logged and fixed as **FIX-217** at the user's direction: probing the family showed `UpdateMany`, `SoftDeleteMany` and `RestoreMany` carried it too — the write half of each was always guarded (`WHERE 1 = 0`), the trailing re-fetch was not. `HardDeleteMany` returns only an error and was never affected.

**Next:** **27.7** (supporting surface) — the remaining path to the 27.1–27.7 checkpoint, and independent of everything above it.

**The 27.5 → 27.6 window, recorded rather than left implicit.** `operations.upsert_many` defaults to `true`, so the producer ships on every table while the three consumer arms are 27.6's content. Two permissive defaults are live contract violations until then: `mapOpToAction` emits a **new wire action `"upsert_many"`** (against PRD §21.2 / §28, which say the op adds none) and cache `dispatchMutation` falls through to `return nil`, so batched upserts **do not invalidate**. The third was a real leak introduced here and fixed here — `emitCreate` did not list `UpsertMany`, so a table redacting a create-input field while generating only `UpsertMany` fell to the non-fail-closed default and published the whole slice un-redacted (the FIX-207 class); it now fails closed. Whether to ship the remaining window or default the flag off until 27.6 is a user call, and the default is PRD-normative.
